                                         The Agentic Scientist: Orchestrating Coding
                                         Agents for Automated Scientific (Co-)Discovery
                                         Robert Tjarko Lange1,* , Luca Grillotti1,* , Yutaro Yamada1,* , Rujikorn Charakorn1 and David Ha1
                                         1 Sakana AI, * Equal Contribution.




                                         Modern coding agents are fundamentally reshaping the role of human input for computational research:
                                         they write code, launch in silico experiments, analyze their results, and autonomously operate with
                                         ever-increasing time horizons. Despite these developments, fully autonomous research agents suffer
                                         from a limited ‘ideation radius’ and myopic hill-climbing search dynamics. To bridge this gap, we




arXiv:2610.18933v1 [cs.AI] 13 Oct 2026
                                         introduce The Agentic Scientist, an end-to-end distributed pipeline for human researchers to
                                         conduct computational research alongside modern coding agents. Building on its predecessor (The
                                         AI Scientist, Lu et al., 2026), our system decomposes research into task drafting, idea generation,
                                         planning, sandboxed experimentation, result aggregation, baseline and ablation construction, post-hoc
                                         analysis, and report writing. Importantly, each step in the pipeline is powered by the next generation of
                                         headless coding agents and leverages agentic self-verification. The overall research process is driven by a
                                         meta-controller that manages parallel research agents and enforces rubric verification at each stage. This
                                         design enables the same orchestration layer to be reused across heterogeneous domains with different
                                         execution environments, agentic coding harnesses, and evaluation metrics. Our system can be used across
                                         a spectrum of autonomy: either as a co-scientist tool, or to perform end-to-end scientific investigation.
                                         Within the co-scientist setting, we leveraged a development version of our system to submit a single
                                         research paper, which got accepted for publication at ALIFE 2026 (Yamada et al., 2026). We further
                                         apply The Agentic Scientist fully autonomously to open problem-solving tasks in machine learning,
                                         and demonstrate the system’s capability of scientific discovery across different domains of computational
                                         research, including: a hypernetwork-based inductive bias for rapid Transformer adaptation and a
                                         meta-trained evolution strategy that matches or exceeds CMA-ES on 5 of 7 unseen Brax control tasks.
                                         Our ablations suggest that reliable scientific autonomy depends not only on base model capabilities, but
                                         also on the cross-agent orchestration layer. We open-source The Agentic Scientist and its figure
                                         generation tool, autoillustrate, to aid collective development of automated (co-)scientist systems:
                                                             Agentic Scientist                https://github.com/SakanaAI/AI-Scientist-v3

                 The Agentic Scientist: Orchestrating Coding Agents for Automated Scientific (Co-)Discovery



1. Introduction
Large Language Model (LLM)-based tool-calling agents can now complete increasingly long coding
tasks with minimal human intervention (Kwa et al., 2025). Computational research, however,
is arguably more than a single long coding task: a practical system supporting human scientists
must generate hypotheses, execute controlled experiments, validate claims, iterate on ideas, and
communicate the results. Existing AI scientist systems demonstrate parts of this loop (Jansen et al.,
2025; Karpathy, 2026; Lu et al., 2024b; Schmidgall et al., 2025; Tang et al., 2025; Yamada et al., 2025),
but myopic hill-climbing can make agents behave merely as flexible hyperparameter optimization
engines (Ferreira et al., 2026; Van Stein et al., 2024).
Here, we tackle the systems-level gap between an agent that writes code and a system that can conduct
reliable research. We introduce The Agentic Scientist (Figure 1), a human-steerable, agentic
scaffold for end-to-end computational research. The workflow drafts a task specification from a text
description, coordinates parallel sandboxed workers, accumulates retrospective evidence, and writes
a manuscript grounded in the results. During the investigation phase, parallel workers revise scientific
hypotheses in response to implementation failures, measured outcomes, and shared performance
notes. The resulting artifacts seed subsequent experiments and remain available for retrospective
analysis, ablation design, and manuscript writing. Predecessors of our system (Table 1) relied on initial
implementation directions and loose assessment of the idea implementation. This could result in weak
experiment validation and overfitting. The Agentic Scientist, on the other hand, uses self-drafted
rubrics-based verification and CLI agents for broader code edits and information protocols.

Table 1 | Comparison of Sakana AI’s AI Scientist Frameworks. The Agentic Scientist enhances
previous versions with rubric-based verification and the option for co-scientist enhancement.

Feature                Codebase               Execution             Parallel     Rubric-based              Human Evaluation
                       Drafting               Workflow            Experiments   Self-Verification              Result
 AI Scientist-v1      Topic-Specific        Linear (Aider)            ✗                ✗                      Not Submitted
 AI Scientist-v2     Domain-General       Tree-Based (AIDE)           ✓                ✗             Conference Workshop Acceptance
 Agentic Scientist   Domain-General    Beam Search (CLI Agents)       ✓                ✓            Co-Scientist Conference Acceptance


A development version of our system was used to automate parts of the research process on Error
Diffusion (Fujita, 2026; Kaneko, 2000). Afterwards, we submitted a single paper which was accepted
as an ALIFE 2026 talk (Yamada et al., 2026). We further demonstrate our framework across three
Machine Learning research directions: our system discovers composition and optimization biases
for hypernetwork-based LLM adaptation (Charakorn et al., 2026). The Agentic Scientist also
extends research on neural network-parametrized meta-black-box optimization (Ma et al., 2025),
yielding an optimizer now integrated in evosax (Lange, 2023). Finally, our system manages to
iteratively improve an RL-based LLM fine-tuning program by combining recent algorithmic advances.
All of these demonstrate the broad application of the system to enhance scientific knowledge beyond
mere hill-climbing applications. Our main contributions are:
  1. The Agentic Scientist, a (co-)scientist orchestration scaffold for human-steerable ideation,
     parallel execution, shared memory, rubric-based verification, and paper manuscript writing.
  2. Co-scientist paper conference acceptance. A paper created with assistance by The Agentic
     Scientist on backpropagation-free error diffusion learning algorithms was accepted at ALIFE
     2026 (Yamada et al., 2026) after limited human edits and verification.
  3. Cross-domain & harness validation spanning long-context LLM adaptation, evolutionary
     optimization, RL fine-tuning, and 5 additional domains. Across various tasks, model harnesses,
     and base models, our system managed to generate high-quality grounded research manuscripts.
  4. autoillustrate, an agentic pipeline for editable scientific figure generation that outperforms
     PaperBanana on PaperBananaBench (37.5 vs. 25.0 overall score, Zhu et al., 2026a).

                The Agentic Scientist: Orchestrating Coding Agents for Automated Scientific (Co-)Discovery



2. Background & Related Work
End-to-end automated scientific discovery. Research on LLM-driven science increasingly targets
complete workflows rather than isolated steps (Si et al., 2025). The AI Scientist-v1 intro-
duced a closed loop for proposing hypotheses, running experiments, analyzing results, and drafting
manuscripts (Lu et al., 2024b), while The AI Scientist-v2 removed human-provided code templates
and introduced progressive agentic tree search (Yamada et al., 2025). Ultimately, these developments
combined with base LLM capabilities have led to end-to-end autonomous systems capable of producing
workshop-level research content (AutoScience AI, 2025; Intology AI, 2025; Lu et al., 2026). Agent
Laboratory and AI-Researcher similarly connect literature analysis, experimentation, and report
writing, with different opportunities for human feedback (Schmidgall et al., 2025; Tang et al., 2025).
CodeScientist combines literature and code search with code review, replication, and external assess-
ment (Jansen et al., 2025), while CycleResearcher inserts automated review into the loop (Weng
et al., 2025) and the AI co-scientist emphasizes scientist-guided hypothesis development (Gottweis
et al., 2026). These systems vary in autonomy, search, and validation; here our focus is the reusable
plus scalable orchestration and evidence layer that supports heterogeneous coding agents.

Hill-Climbing via Program Evolution. A complementary line casts discovery as search over exe-
cutable artifacts. FunSearch showed that language-model-guided program search can yield mathe-
matical discoveries (Romera-Paredes et al., 2024); AlphaEvolve broadened evolutionary code search
to algorithms and computational infrastructure (Novikov et al., 2025), and ShinkaEvolve targets open-
ended, sample-efficient program evolution (Lange et al., 2026). At a lighter weight, autoresearch
uses coding agents to repeatedly edit and evaluate a training program under a fixed budget (Karpathy,
2026), motivating direct comparison with classical hyperparameter optimization (Ferreira et al.,
2026). ALE-Agent (Imajuku et al., 2025) demonstrated that simple beam search at sufficient scale
provides a competitive harness for program optimization. Across these approaches, the search
unit is a program under a pre-specified objective. Our scaffold uses such executable search within a
broader hypothesis-to-report process: it broadens the scope of application by automating task drafting,
validation rubric generation, and supporting objective creation.

Benchmarking Research Agent Capabilities. Research-agent benchmarks separate several capa-
bilities that a single final score can obscure. SWE-agent (Yang et al., 2024b) established that the
agent–computer interface strongly affects real-world coding performance. MLAgentBench evalu-
ates iterative machine-learning experimentation (Huang et al., 2024), whereas MLE-Bench covers
longer-horizon engineering workflows involving data preparation, debugging, training, and opti-
mization (Chan et al., 2025); subsequent work studies how search strategy affects exploration and
generalization in this setting (Toledo et al., 2025). At the research level, PaperBench measures
replication of published AI work (Starace et al., 2025), MLR-Bench targets open-ended machine-
learning research (Chen et al., 2025), and EXP-Bench isolates whether agents can conduct valid
AI experiments (Kon et al., 2026). Lu et al. (2024b) introduced an LLM-driven pipeline for paper
reviewing, which was later validated on research papers published after the training data cutoff date
(Lu et al., 2026). Here, we make use of such automated proxy AI reviews and additionally report
external reviews of the co-produced ALIFE submission as complementary evidence.

Verification and Grounding. Verification and communication are becoming explicit parts of agent
design. Rubric rewards can train AI co-scientists and provide feedback in domains without verifiable
objectives (Goel et al., 2026; Gunjal et al., 2026). Here, rubrics do not train the coding-agent
backbone; instead, they serve as runtime contracts that ground each hypothesis to implementation
checks and empirical evidence. The The Agentic Scientist manuscript drafting runs additional
checks for facts and figures outlined in the paper. We discuss further related work in Section A.


                                                                                                             3
                               The Agentic Scientist: Orchestrating Coding Agents for Automated Scientific (Co-)Discovery



3. The Agentic Scientist: An Orchestration Layer for Research Agents
The Agentic Scientist is a versatile agentic orchestrator scaffold for end-to-end computational
research campaigns (Figure 1). A campaign begins with a task specification, which researchers can
provide directly or generate from a research brief with a drafting agent. Then, the campaign proceeds
through three phases: parallel investigation, retrospective evidence generation, and grounded paper
writing. Each phase runs as a separate process and feeds the next: the selected program and
experimental artifacts produced by the first phase determine which baselines, ablations, and post-hoc
analyses to run; the resulting evidence then grounds the manuscript. Finally, we leverage the previously
introduced AI reviewer (Lu et al., 2026) to provide proxy evaluation scores for the manuscripts.

3.1. Research Task Setup
Before idea execution, The Agentic Scientist requires an executable task specification that defines
the research goal and its evaluation. This can either be specified manually or drafted agentically:

review agent assesses the design and repairs any issues. The drafting agent then implements the
initial program, the search and final evaluation programs, and the task contract, while pinning
dependencies and staging the required data. The harness checks the contract and runs smoke tests
of the evaluation programs before the alignment judge assesses scientific fidelity and verifies that
the search and final evaluators are independent and use disjoint data. In containerized runs, the
production check additionally requires the initial program to complete the full search workload to
ensure operational feasibility. Failed scaffolds are repaired, and only versions that pass all checks
proceed to parallel investigation.

                 The Agentic Scientist: Orchestrating Coding Agents for Automated Scientific (Co-)Discovery



3.2. Parallel Investigation
The idea investigation phase spends a fixed budget of containerized research cycles across parallel
workers, each exploring different hypotheses in a separate workspace while sharing distilled knowledge
(Figure 3). Multiple agent-based workers explore ideas in parallel, each advancing an independent
sequence of research cycles. These workers lease cycles from a synchronized queue, maintain separate
run directories and worker-local best search score programs, which resemble a per-worker beam
search. A cycle starts with the worker importing the shared memory and the other workers’ distilled
conclusions, and ends with the worker publishing new memory entries and updated conclusions to all
other workers. Communication occurs at cycle boundaries, while logs and program artifacts remain in
worker directories. At regular intervals, optional context pruning archives the histories and memory
before resetting them, so that accumulated context does not bias subsequent cycles; the best program
is carried into the next round to preserve progress. After parallel execution, the aggregator merges
worker histories and selects the best correct program found across workers. This selected program
becomes the input to the retrospective evidence-generation phase.

Execution agent. The accepted contract, evaluator, task files, and the worker’s best program are
staged into an experiment workspace. The execution agent creates append-only candidate variants and
follows an edit–evaluate–verify loop within a fixed evaluation budget, producing metrics, correctness
checks, and rubric evidence. Two distinct gates are then applied: an all-rubric-pass attempt marks
the contract complete, whereas updating the worker’s best program requires a correct candidate with
a strictly higher score; rubric completion and updates to the worker’s best program are therefore
independent decisions. All logged idea provenance and attempt outcomes are retained and later
condensed to guide interpretation during paper writing.

3.4. Retrospective Evidence Generation
This phase studies the selected program from the investigation phase in depth: (1) it runs con-
trolled baselines and ablations that isolate the idea effects, and (2) it derives post-hoc analyses that
characterize the behavior of the method on the considered task.

                                                                                                                                                5
                                 The Agentic Scientist: Orchestrating Coding Agents for Automated Scientific (Co-)Discovery



Controlled baselines and ablations. The selected program is first instrumented with additional
metrics, such as extra scores and time series data, so that every generated baseline and ablation can be
compared in detail on common ground. Given the code diff between the selected and initial programs,
an agent then proposes baselines and ablations with explicit hypotheses and implementation plans,
where each ablation must isolate an innovation introduced by the selected program. A separate
agent filters these proposals for relevance, feasibility, and single-change scope. Each validated plan
receives an isolated workspace and follows an agent-guided edit–evaluate–verify loop that modifies
the selected program to implement the intended change and score it against the evaluator, alongside
the unchanged selected and initial programs. The evaluator can be run for multiple seeds to make
comparisons statistically meaningful, and their summaries and metrics are aggregated across seeds,
ablations, and baselines by task.

Post-hoc analysis. The post-hoc pipeline proposes, filters, executes, and aggregates diagnostics that
characterize the behavior of the frozen selected program on the considered task. Diagnostics may
run new inference or analyze existing artifacts, such as checkpoints and logs. Programmatic checks
reject proposals that retrain or otherwise alter the selected program, or require unavailable artifacts.
When no usable checkpoint exists, an optional bootstrap stage enables checkpoint saving and reruns
the evaluator once; all subsequent analyses keep the resulting version frozen. The resulting post-hoc
analyses, together with the baseline and ablation results, form the evidence that the paper writing
phase turns into empirical claims.

3.5. Grounded Paper Writing
The paper writing phase converts the outputs of the preceding phases into a manuscript through an
artifact-grounded workflow that reviews and audits claims made in the paper (Figure 4).

judgment and feeds any failures back to the writer agent for revision. The validation checks cover struc-

3.6. autoillustrate: Conceptual Diagram Generation
autoillustrate is designed around two requirements: scientists should be able to modify concep-
tual figures, and they should represent the methodology faithfully. To meet these requirements, the
pipeline first produces an editable draw.io diagram to ground the figure’s structure, which is later
exported and augmented using a separate image generation model. As illustrated in Figure 5, the
concept generator runs a six-phase pipeline that turns a method brief into a multi-panel figure whose
layout source remains explicitly editable. Unless stated otherwise, all papers generated by our system
in this work have their main conceptual figure generated by autoillustrate.

Editable panel generation. The pipeline begins with a planning agent that reads the staged source
and instructions and describes the panels within one top-level configurable layout. A styling agent then
defines a shared color palette and typography specification to keep independently rendered panels
consistent. Rendering agents create the panels independently and in parallel as editable draw.io
diagrams. Each agent relies on a template to define the panel topology and a Python generator script
to compute coordinates and check bounding boxes for overlap. Visuals that draw.io cannot produce
remain as placeholder boxes. When possible, a generated matplotlib script fills these boxes; the
enhancement stage handles any remaining placeholders. A vision-model audit then evaluates each
panel for rendering fidelity, factual accuracy, and sufficient detail. When the inspection detects a
defect, the pipeline edits the source, rebuilds the panel, and repeats the audit.

Composition, enhancement, and verification. After all panels pass, the pipeline composes them
into a single editable draw.io source and exports a raster version. An image generation model
restyles the raster export, after which a final verification step compares the enhanced candidate
with the editable reference and repeats the checks defined during panel inspection. Any discrepancy
triggers another source-editing, export, enhancement, and verification pass. After several repair
passes, the pipeline returns the enhanced candidate with the strongest verification results. If no
candidate passes verification, the pipeline returns the raster export from the editable source. Because
the pipeline preserves this source throughout the process, scientists can revise the content or layout
directly without regenerating the figure. We provide additional details in Section E.




Figure 5 | autoillustrate pipeline, generated by autoillustrate itself. (A) Six phases turn a
document and an instruction into an editable draw.io figure: LLM sub-agents decide content, while
deterministic Python owns geometry and the generative image model only restyles. (B) In Phase 2,
panels are rendered from a combination of automatically-generated templates whose coordinates are
determined by LLM-generated Python code. (C) In Phases 5–6, the enhanced candidate with the best
audit results is selected; if none is acceptable, the deterministic render is used as a fallback.

4. AI Co-Scientist and Conference Paper Submission
We validated a development version of our system within a co-scientist setting: The Agentic Scien-
tist was used to automate parts of the research process targeting the extension of a backpropagation-
free neural network training method. After thorough human verification and manuscript edits, we
submitted a single paper to ALIFE 2026, which was accepted after external peer review as a main
conference talk. We emphasize that this single submitted ALIFE paper was produced by human
researchers using The Agentic Scientist as a tool, and not submitted as an unaltered system
output. The human authors remain responsible for its methods, claims, authorship, and disclosure. In
this section, our focus is to describe the process by which experimental outputs became a submitted
manuscript and were subsequently revised for the camera-ready version (Figure 6).




Figure 6 | Camera-ready ALIFE 2026 paper after the single submitted manuscript was accepted. The
full manuscript and additional details can be found in Section D.


4.1. A Human-Supervised Research Workflow
The project began with a human-selected research direction: investigating how Error Diffusion (ED)
could be extended beyond its earlier demonstrations (Fujita, 2026; Kaneko, 2000). ED leverages
a three-factor learning rule (presynaptic activity, postsynaptic activity, and a globally broadcast
error signal) originally studied in a dual-stream setting with paired excitatory (E) and inhibitory (I)
neurons. We chose to illuminate this research direction together with The Agentic Scientist
since it investigates the following question: how can classical biologically-inspired learning algorithms
be revived with modern deep learning techniques?
Human researchers specified the scientific objective and the constraints for a valid implementation.
Within that contract, The Agentic Scientist proposed candidates, implemented training pro-
cedures, ran experiments, inspected failures, and generated plots. The human researchers selected
benchmarks (see creation process overview in Figure 7) and specified initial rubrics: A numerically

baseline, and rubrics; supported the experiment domain transition; verified results, references, and
claims before submission; and incorporated reviewer feedback into the camera-ready paper.


strong result was not treated as evidence of progress unless its implementation also satisfied the
rubric constraints. For example, one rubric prohibited hidden credit assignment through automatic
differentiation or transposed forward weights. This required the positive and negative streams to
remain distinct dual-stream, and specified the intended local information available to an update.

4.2. Summary of Discovery and Dynamics
The Agentic Scientist first extended ED from binary to multiclass classification: during the
parallel investigation phase, The Agentic Scientist developed modulo error routing, a structured
mechanism for assigning multi-class output errors to hidden units. This enabled multiclass learning
while preserving the dual-stream, Dale-constrained architecture, in which weight signs remain fixed
throughout training. Subsequent experiments focused on robustifying learning dynamics: The
Agentic Scientist identified layer-specific sigmoid widths, batch-centered error signals, and
asymmetric E/I initialization as important stabilizers, raising accuracy to 96.7% on MNIST (LeCun
et al., 1998) and 61.7% on CIFAR-10 (Krizhevsky et al., 2009). Ablations further showed that the
individual contributions were task-dependent: widened sigmoids were critical on MNIST, whereas
batch-centering became substantially more important on CIFAR-10.
At this stage, human researchers transferred the best-performing ED implementation from the MNIST
and CIFAR-10 experiments to the RL setting, and prepared the Brax (Freeman et al., 2021) and
Craftax (Matthews et al., 2024) environments. For RL experiments, an additional methodological
constraint was added to the rubrics: the method was required to retain the PPO objective (Schulman
et al., 2017), while using the gradient of the PPO loss with respect to the logits as the output-level
error signal for Error Diffusion. Within this constraint, The Agentic Scientist adapted the
dual-stream ED mechanism to PPO and explored the resulting ED-PPO variants. ED-PPO achieved
performance comparable to standard baselines on several Brax tasks, although its performance was
less consistent on Humanoid and Craftax (see figures in ALIFE paper manuscript; Section C.3).
Post-hoc analyses linked these results to the dynamics of credit assignment: local update signals
weakened substantially in earlier layers, while training shifted E/I ratios toward balance. In summary,
the results obtained demonstrate that ED generalizes beyond its original binary-classification setting.
The mechanisms required for effective learning depend on task difficulty and domain.

4.3. Human Result Verification & Manuscript Edits
Before submission to ALIFE, researchers verified the generated research artifacts. This included check-
ing that the generated implementations matched the scientific hypotheses being tested, confirming
that the reported tables and figures accurately reflected results produced by the corresponding code,
and revising the manuscript where the generated text overstated or inaccurately characterized the

evidence. For example, human researchers added appropriate statistical analyses and, when the
results showed comparable performance, revised claims such as “ED-PPO significantly exceeds BP-PPO”
to “ED-PPO performs on par with BP-PPO”. Additionally, all references in the BibTeX bibliography were

These included the reference to the original ED work, which is available only through an archived
webpage. The generated manuscript was treated as an intermediate research artifact, which was
modified by human researchers, improving the writing of several sections (e.g., future work, etc.).
We then submitted this single paper to the ALIFE 2026 conference.

4.4. Peer Review, Camera-Ready Paper Revisions
This submission process illustrates the AI co-scientist approach enabled by our system: outputs were
not just optimized code or isolated experimental reports, but a coherent working paper making a
scientific claim, positioning it relative to prior work, and presenting evidence across multiple domains.
We emphasize that only a single paper was submitted after our careful manual verification.
The subsequent ALIFE peer review process provides external evidence for the quality of the co-created
paper submission. Overall, the paper was accepted as a talk, with individual recommendations of
Strong ACCEPT, Weak REJECT, and Weak ACCEPT:
Reviewer 1 framed the work as a relevant artificial-life contribution, as it evaluates the consequences
of a biological assumption, Dale’s principle, and found the methods and results technically sound.
Reviewer 2 highlighted that the neuroscience connection of the work was interesting but questioned
whether the paper would better fit a neural network venue. Additionally, they asked for clearer
explanations of notation, matrix conventions, modulo indexing, and technical details.
Reviewer 3 was positive and requested more intuition about ED updates, non-negative activations,
per-neuron error signals, baseline comparisons, ED-PPO architecture, and the meaning of gradient
attenuation without backpropagation.
We afterward used this feedback to enhance the camera-ready version of the manuscript: the post-
acceptance changes are best characterized as targeted scientific clarifications rather than a change
in the underlying study. In particular, the camera-ready version tightens several claims about prior
ED results, adds Dale-compliant reinforcement-learning results, corrects the Error Diffusion citation
context, clarifies that Direct Feedback Alignment (Nøkland, 2016) routes errors through fixed random
matrices, and cleans up the dual-stream architecture description and ED update equation. It also
replaces potentially misleading gradient language with “proxy” or “local surrogate” gradient termi-
nology, refines the biological interpretation of E/I balance, repositions the discussion of Craftax and
the dual-stream parameter overhead, and fixes typographic details such as quotation marks. These
edits make the paper more precise about biological plausibility and local learning while leaving the
empirical evidence and overall narrative intact. Table 2 summarizes human intervention throughout
the manuscript-development process. In summary, this study provides evidence of The Agentic
Scientist being capable of supporting researchers to explore unorthodox AI topics across the entire
research process.

5. Evaluating The Agentic Scientist Across Tasks, Harnesses, and LLMs
We evaluate The Agentic Scientist across five manually prepared research tasks that span
optimization, representation learning, and predictive modelling. Each campaign starts from a task
specification with different research objectives, starting points, and search iteration resources (see

Task, harness, and model sweep. We run the task sweep across four agent harnesses: Codex,
Claude Code, Pi, and OpenCode (Anomaly Innovations, 2026; Anthropic, 2026; Earendil Works, 2026;
OpenAI, 2026). Codex and Claude Code each use four assigned models, while Pi and OpenCode each
use three, all at high reasoning effort (Table 10). Each run uses 20 idea investigation iterations and
concludes with a written manuscript. Here, we focus on aggregated insights and provide more details
on task-specific settings and discoveries can be found in Section B.1.

metric: For each generated manuscript, five separate calls to o4-mini-2025-04-16 review the

pair. A sixth call acts as an area chair to synthesize the reviews. The original study in Lu et al. (2026)
validated the reviewer against acceptance decisions for ICLR submissions from OpenReview, including
a post-training-data-cutoff 2025 cohort. We note that the AI reviewer score solely serves as a proxy for
reviewer-perceived paper quality, and not as ground-truth evidence of full scientific correctness.

model developers’ own harnesses offer no consistent advantage: Codex ties Pi with GPT-5.6-Sol, and

      6. Additional Case Studies: Putting The Agentic Scientist to work
     We further evaluate The Agentic Scientist on three case studies: Language model adaptation
     (Section 6.1) through hypernetworks (Charakorn et al., 2026), evolutionary optimization (Section 6.2)
     through meta-learned neural network parametrizations (Lange et al., 2023a), and language model
     RL fine-tuning (Section 6.3) on mathematical reasoning tasks.

      6.1. Open-Ended Architecture Research with The Agentic Scientist: Discovering Query-
            Conditioned Composition for Hypernetwork-Based LLM Adaptation
      In this experiment, we explore how The Agentic Scientist can autonomously write an insightful
      scientific paper after successfully investigating a specific topic. As a case study, we choose the recently
      published hypernetwork-based LLM adaptation work from Charakorn et al. (2026), Doc-to-LoRA.
      Unlike the other settings, The Agentic Scientist is given the Doc-to-LoRA paper’s LaTeX source
      and its public code repository as the starting point.

     Figure 10 | (Left) Search trajectories for Doc-to-LoRA scientist runs given 40 idea iteration budget
     with Codex + GPT-5.6-Sol, Claude + Opus-5, and Pi + DeepSeek-V4-Pro on the multi-NIAH task.
     Both Codex and Claude runs improve steadily on the task. (Right) Exact-match accuracy is shown as
     a heatmap grouped by the number of test sample needles. Red crosses indicate invalid iterations.

     Problem formulation. Doc-to-LoRA introduces a hypernetwork that outputs low-rank adaptation
     (LoRA) matrices for a target LLM given a textual document. The generated LoRA serves as a weight-
     distilled knowledge bank and can be used to update the target LLM for document-based question
     answering without the need to place the documents in the LLM’s context window. To handle documents
     beyond the context length of its encoder model, Doc-to-LoRA splits long documents into chunks
     and generates one LoRA independently for each chunk. The standard approach concatenates the
     per-chunk low-rank factors into a higher-rank LoRA. However, naively combining multiple LoRAs can
     cause interference between several facts induced by different chunks. Here, we focus on multi-needle-
     in-a-haystack (multi-NIAH) retrieval as a proxy task: each context contains one to four named facts,
     but the prompt asks for only one target needle’s four-digit number. To be successful at this task, a
     trained hypernetwork must avoid multi-LoRA interference via compositional LoRA designs.

     Needle-in-a-haystack task setup. Any modification proposed by The Agentic Scientist is lim-
     ited to architectural or training-related edits given a fixed encoder Gemma-2-2b-it model (Gemma
     Team, 2024), fixed data generation, fixed evaluation scripts, and a 3-hour single-GPU training time
     budget. The evaluation spans five context-length bins from short up to 65,536 tokens, with needles
     randomly placed at random positions. Given the resulting Doc-2-LoRA weights, 𝜃, the investigation
     target for The Agentic Scientist is the exact-match accuracy on the queried number:
                                     1 ∑︁
                max score( 𝜃) =               EM𝑑 ( 𝜃); 𝐷 = {128, 8192, 16384, 32768, 65536},
                  𝜃                | 𝐷| 𝑑 ∈ 𝐷

                                                                                                                                                                                    13
                                               The Agentic Scientist: Orchestrating Coding Agents for Automated Scientific (Co-)Discovery



where EM is exact-match accuracy and 𝐷 is the set of evaluation context-length bins.

The Agentic Scientist search dynamics. We ran The Agentic Scientist for 40 idea-
investigation steps with three model-harness combinations (Figure 10). The Claude and Codex
variants both reach near-optimal scores, with Claude improving faster. In contrast, Pi + DeepSeek-
v4-Pro’s run shows fewer but larger jumps and plateaus below 50%. In the Claude run, the generated
report organizes the proposals by the mechanism each one tests: architecture redesign, auxiliary
training objectives, LoRA merge weighting, and query-conditioned composition. The key discoveries
are as follows: redesigning the architecture or adding auxiliary objectives yields little gain, whereas
query-conditioning the LoRA composition alone raises accuracy to 90.8%. Adding query-relevance
filtering with delimiter-aligned chunk boundaries reaches 98.0% (Figure 11). The generated paper
therefore concludes that the bottleneck is not adapter-generation capacity or the training loss: multi-
needle Doc-to-LoRA retrieval is a routing problem, in which the system must decide, based on the
query, which chunk’s adapter to activate when merging multiple adapters. The full paper written by
The Agentic Scientist and its AI review are presented in Section C.1.

Figure 11 | Doc-to-LoRA composition mechanism ablations figure in the generated report (Section C.1).
(a) Hypernetwork’s head redesign, auxiliary objective, and unconditional merging stay at or below the
51.7% exact-match accuracy, while query-conditioned composition reaches 98.0%. (b) Starting from
the query-blind (unconditional) merge, query-dependent merging provides a significant improvement;
relevance-based text placement and delimiter-aligned chunking close the remaining gap.

Discovered Inductive Biases. The Agentic Scientist demonstrates that naively composing every
generated adapter makes the target LLM conflate multiple facts. The main failure mode is responding
with a distractor number rather than the queried one. The successful idea is to make the adapter
merging query-conditioned: The Agentic Scientist proposes an interaction score between the
query and each chunk that selects the right adapter at composition time, without adding trainable
parameters or changing the training objective. The Agentic Scientist then adds two smaller
but complementary structural changes: relevance-based text processing and delimiter-aligned text
chunking. Relevance-based text processing removes the noise tokens around the token span most
relevant to the query, so the adapter is generated from the queried fact, instead of the neighboring
haystack. Delimiter-aligned text chunking keeps each needle sentence intact before the query-
conditioned address is computed. We note that the The Agentic Scientist demonstrated that this
inductive bias works well with the multi-needle-in-a haystack task. Future work should investigate the
generalization of the discovered mechanisms beyond this synthetic long-context retrieval task.




                                                                                                                                                                                           14
                The Agentic Scientist: Orchestrating Coding Agents for Automated Scientific (Co-)Discovery



6.2. Open-Ended Algorithm Discovery with The Agentic Scientist:
      Meta-Evolving CMA-Anchored Learned Evolution Strategies
Black-box optimization (BBO) is an established target for automating algorithm discovery (Ma
et al., 2025): compact update rules determine sampling distributions, solution recombination, and
covariance adaptation. But coordinating these mechanisms remains difficult (Shala et al., 2020).
One line of prior work focused on learning attention-parametrized evolutionary algorithms (EA)
through meta-BBO (Faldor et al., 2025a; Lange et al., 2023a,b). Here, we ask whether The Agentic
Scientist can improve the meta-optimizer program itself (i.e., perform ‘meta-meta-BBO’).

Task setup. The goal of this research campaign is to design a better procedure for obtaining a
learned evolutionary algorithm. Each program proposed by The Agentic Scientist is judged by
how well its learned EA generalizes to unseen tasks. Evaluation proceeds in two steps: (1) the EA’s
neural network parameters are meta-trained on a set of synthetic tasks, and (2) the resulting frozen
parameters are evaluated on a separate held-out task. For meta-training, we use CMA-ES (Hansen
and Ostermeier, 2001): at each generation, a random subset of BBOB (Hansen et al., 2021) tasks is
sampled, and the program runs optimization for a population of EA parameters. The best task-specific
objective values reached by an EA instance are aggregated into a single meta-training score. After
meta-training completes, the final CMA-ES mean is frozen and evaluated on a validation task: the
learned EA algorithm evolves a linear policy on a robotic control task (Brax Ant-v1, Freeman et al.,
2021). This evaluation tests the generalization capabilities to new tasks and optimization budgets
(2,000 generations with a population size of 256). The agent-level search score is defined as:

                     score = (AUCcandidate − AUCinitial ) /(AUCCMA-ES − AUCinitial ) ,

where AUC is the mean of the 2,000-point best-so-far return curve. The synthetic tasks are exclusively
used to train the weights of an The Agentic Scientist EA proposal, while the validation Ant
evaluation measures task generalization and scores optimizer programs across agent iterations.




Figure 12 | Search trajectories for 30 idea iterations with GPT-5.6-Sol + Codex, Opus-5 + Claude,
DeepSeek V4 Flash + Pi, and Kimi K3 + OpenCode. Dots are individual Ant search-validation
evaluations and step curves are the cumulative best.

The Agentic Scientist search dynamics. All four considered The Agentic Scientist
configurations obtain a normalized search score above one, indicating that each achieves a higher
AUC than CMA-ES on the Ant-v1 task (Figure 12). Although the final GPT-5.6-sol + Codex and Kimi
K3 + OpenCode candidates improve AUC over CMA-ES, their final Ant-v1 returns remain low: Opus-5
+ Claude and DeepSeek V4 Flash + Pi achieve the highest AUCs and terminal returns, whereas
Kimi K3 yields the smallest AUC gain. Claude’s highest-scoring candidate exceeds the dual-timescale
diagonal innovation-memory strategy discovered by Codex in both AUC and terminal return. Next,


                                                                                                             15
                The Agentic Scientist: Orchestrating Coding Agents for Automated Scientific (Co-)Discovery



we present the discoveries made by The Agentic Scientist using the previous Claude + Opus 5
setup. The detailed The Agentic Scientist paper is available in Section C.2.

Strategy selected for the generated paper. The system discovered CMA-Anchored LearnedES, which
uses a separable-CMA (Ros and Hansen, 2008) distribution 𝑥 = 𝑚 + 𝜎𝑑 ⊙ 𝑧 and additionally augments
the algorithm with three small shared MLPs containing 364 meta-evolved parameters: A permutation-
equivariant Deep Sets (Zaheer et al., 2017) module adjusts CMA recombination weights; global
gates modulate mean and cumulative-step-size updates; and a coordinate-shared gate modulates
diagonal covariance adaptation. All learned outputs are bounded multiplicative residuals in [ 12 , 2];
zero network outputs recover the separable-CMA update equations before the fixed sampling and
re-expansion mechanisms described below. The agent additionally implements fixed, horizon-free
mechanisms around these learned gates (see Section C.2). These mechanisms are program structure
rather than meta-evolved weights. Notably, the re-expansion cannot fire during the 50-generation
BBOB inner loop, indicating an interplay between Ant-v1 validation and idea investigation.

Post-search transfer across Brax tasks. The manuscript drafted by The Agentic Scientist
mentions the possibility of limited meta-transfer to some unseen tasks. Therefore, we test the
selected Claude, Codex, and Pi programs on eight Brax-v1 environments using 16 new shared seeds:
seven unseen environments and Ant as the search-validation reference environment. The task setup
differs from the meta-held-out setting: the test evaluation uses a zero-initialized affine–tanh policy,
population 256, 2,000 generations, initial standard deviation 0.05, and 500-step episodes.




Figure 13 | Post-search evaluation across eight Brax-v1 legacy-spring environments. Curves show the
mean best-so-far return over 16 shared seeds; shaded regions denote one standard error. The grey
Ant panel marks the search-validation reference environment, evaluated here with new seeds and the
reduced transfer-policy interface; the other seven environments were unseen during agentic search.
While no method dominates on all environments (Figure 13; Table 5) the Opus-5 + Claude Code
candidate matches or exceeds the mean terminal return of CMA-ES on 6 out of 8 environments and
falls short on HalfCheetah and Hopper. The DeepSeek V4 Flash + Pi candidate has the highest sample
mean on HalfCheetah, but its margin over CMA-ES is smaller than the Pi candidate’s standard error.
The GPT-5.6-Sol + Codex candidate leads on Reacher and Walker2d. We have released the discovered
CMA-Anchored LearnedES optimizer for further study in evosax v0.3.2 (Lange, 2023).


                                                                                                             16
                     The Agentic Scientist: Orchestrating Coding Agents for Automated Scientific (Co-)Discovery



6.3. RL-Based LLM Fine-Tuning with The Agentic Scientist:
     Studying the Effects of Agent Choice, Parallelism, and Shared Knowledge
Finally, we also evaluate The Agentic Scientist as a hill-climbing system for Reinforcement
Learning (RL)-based language model fine-tuning. Additionally, we provide several ablations on key
configuration settings of The Agentic Scientist system. In this setting, the system iteratively
improves a training program through algorithmic modifications and hyperparameter tuning.

Task setup. The task consists of optimizing the training procedure of a Qwen2.5-Math model (Yang
et al., 2024a) on the MATH dataset from Hendrycks et al. (2021). We follow the task formulation of Si
et al. (2026), in which each worker is initialized from a baseline Group Relative Policy Optimization
(GRPO, Shao et al., 2024) training pipeline. Our implementation is a re-engineered version of their
reference code, restructured to integrate with the The Agentic Scientist sandbox, logging, and
evaluation interfaces; the learning algorithm itself remains a faithful instantiation of GRPO.

Algorithmic changes in the best programs. We find that the improvements discovered by The
Agentic Scientist combine real algorithmic changes with hyperparameter tuning: The Agentic
Scientist re-implements and combines several known techniques from the recent literature on RL
fine-tuning. These are not independent rediscoveries: The Agentic Scientist had in principle
access to these techniques through web search, and they likely appeared in the underlying model’s
training data. The most common ones are the DAPO “clip-higher” rule (Yu et al., 2025), an asymmetric
PPO clip with a larger upper bound, so that promising low-probability tokens are updated more
strongly; Dr. GRPO loss aggregation (Liu et al., 2025), which divides the loss by a fixed constant
instead of the response length and thus removes the bias against long responses; and GSPO-style
importance ratios (Zheng et al., 2025), that is, a single length-normalized ratio per response instead
of one per token. Smaller changes include removing or simplifying the KL penalty, keeping only
the prompt groups with mixed correct and incorrect answers, and penalizing overly long responses.
Overall, these changes show that hill-climbing over an RL fine-tuning program can not only tune


                                                                                                                                  17
                             The Agentic Scientist: Orchestrating Coding Agents for Automated Scientific (Co-)Discovery



hyperparameters but also select, re-implement, and combine relevant algorithmic ideas from the
recent literature. The Agentic Scientist uses empirical feedback to decide which of these ideas
to keep and how to combine them into training programs with higher accuracy.

Influence of the number of workers. A recurring design choice across our experiments is the
number of parallel workers spawned by the idea investigation stage of The Agentic Scientist
at each iteration. We study its effect on the RL fine-tuning task, running the system with 1, 2, and
4 parallel workers. All worker configurations (Figure 15, Left) improve over the GRPO baseline
validation accuracy (≈ 52.7%), with the 2-worker configuration reaching a validation accuracy of
≈ 66%. Using multiple workers speeds up progress; for instance, the 2-worker variant reaches a
score comparable to the 1-worker peak in less than half the wall-clock time, and effectively shares
information between workers (Figure 15, Right). Nonetheless, the benefit of parallelism is non-
monotonic: using more workers does not necessarily yield better final results, suggesting that a small
amount of parallel exploration injects useful diversity while larger pools can redundantly concentrate
on similar local improvements.

Figure 15 | Search dynamics of The Agentic Scientist on RL fine-tuning across three seeds per

Cross-agent comparison. A central question is how much performance comes from the orchestration
introduced by The Agentic Scientist versus the underlying coding agent. While we partially
address this question in Section 5, we additionally compare Opus-4.7 + Claude Code, GPT-5.6-Sol
+ Codex, and Kimi K3 + Pi for the RL-based LLM fine-tuning task on the idea investigation phase
in isolation. Under this common setting, Pi achieves scores similar to Claude in a faster and more
consistent manner, while reducing the API costs by 75%, and producing programs that generalize
better to the post-search test dataset (Figure 16). Codex, on the other hand, followed the task prompt
instructions (Section G.5) more strictly: it never tuned hyperparameters after algorithmic changes,
limiting the improvement process. Overall, we conclude that the base model can have a significant
impact on the idea investigation phase, including both final performance and search costs.

Rubric-specification sensitivity. We observe a failure mode introduced by structural limitations
of rubric-based verification: rubrics check only the criteria they enumerate, so they cannot catch
violations of constraints that were never stated, and human researchers remain responsible for
specifying tasks precisely and verifying that results respect their full intent. In two of the three Pi
runs, we observed that the agent also modified the test-time evaluation procedure itself (e.g., using


                                                                                                                                                                                              18
                                              The Agentic Scientist: Orchestrating Coding Agents for Automated Scientific (Co-)Discovery



𝑘-sample majority voting instead of the baseline’s pass@1), which inflates the reported validation
accuracy without improving the training algorithm. The task prompt asks the agent to improve the
“training” algorithm, but never explicitly requires a fixed evaluation procedure. No initial rubric item
covers this evaluation-protocol integrity. Neither the evaluator nor the rubric gates flagged these
programs, and we identified them after a manual post-hoc audit. After excluding protocol-violating
programs, Pi still reaches high scores faster and more consistently than all other agents.

Influence of shared knowledge. A second central design choice is the shared knowledge that
connects all workers to a common memory of prior results, run summaries, and failure traces
(Section 3.2). To isolate its contribution, we compare our default 2-worker The Agentic Scientist
to an ablated no shared knowledge variant, where each worker cannot see other workers’ conclusions
and memory. Shared knowledge improves the search process: the default configuration progresses
faster, achieves a higher mean best-program score, and reduces the performance gap between workers
(Figure 17, centre and right). Nonetheless, we observe a substantial amount of inter-run variance.
The two variants reach similar final best scores across workers: the default configuration scores
slightly higher, but the difference is not statistically significant (Figure 17, left). Hence, independent
workers can find strong programs without cooperation at the cost of slower overall runtime. These
results suggest that sharing successful ideas accelerates the search and allows lower-scoring workers
to benefit from improvements found by others.

7. Evaluating autoillustrate on PaperBananaBench




Figure 18 | autoillustrate applied to the Darwin Gödel Machine paper (Zhang et al., 2026a): the
editable diagram (left) and its corresponding enhanced figure (right).
Given a methodology description, autoillustrate produces an editable draw.io diagram and an
enhanced figure derived from it (Figure 18). We evaluate the generated figures on PaperBananaBench
(Zhu et al., 2026a), following the original evaluation protocol and using GPT-6-Astra as the VLM
judge, as it more reliably penalizes small structural errors such as disconnected arrows (Figure 22).
Results with Gemini-3.1-Pro as the judge are reported in Section E.2. Each generated diagram is
compared to its human-designed equivalent with respect to four criteria: Faithfulness, Readability,
Conciseness, and Aesthetics. Each comparison receives 100 points when the generated diagram wins,
50 for a tie, and 0 when it loses. The Overall score prioritizes fidelity and clarity over brevity and
visual polish: a method wins overall if it wins at least one of Faithfulness and Readability without
losing the other; if neither wins, Conciseness and Aesthetics are used to break the tie.
Benchmark results. The Claude-based autoillustrate pipeline achieves the highest Overall
score, 37.5 against 25.0 for the best PaperBanana variant, driven by higher Faithfulness (Table 4). All
autoillustrate variants generate on average more faithful figures, which is crucial for automated
research since it forms the basis of method communication. PaperBanana produces overly detailed
diagrams, with higher chances of small image-generation errors. Conciseness is more challenging
for autoillustrate, as the editable diagram primitives constrain compact and complex visual
abstraction generation. Although autoillustrate remains below the human-reference Overall
score of 50.0, its editable source files allow users to refine generated diagrams. The Codex-based
variant is on par with the Claude-based pipeline on Faithfulness and Conciseness, but scores lower
on Readability and Aesthetics, as it tends to generate more cluttered diagrams. Removing the panel
inspections and final verification critics lowers Faithfulness and Overall scores, as these audits detect
and repair factual inaccuracies with respect to the source methodology.
Limitations: cost and runtime. The autoillustrate variants generally have higher median API
costs per diagram than PaperBanana, assuming raw API pricing. Additionally, autoillustrate’s
median runtime is higher, as producing editable draw.io diagrams requires agents to write XML
templates and Python programs that place elements, avoid collisions, and route arrows precisely,
which demands substantial reasoning. Within autoillustrate, removing the critics approximately
halves runtime, and using low inference effort reduces runtime by a further 30%.

Table 4 | Mean scores on PaperBananaBench, using GPT-6-Astra as VLM judge.
                                                                                                                     Editable

                                                                                                                      source
      autoillustrate       Claude                       32.0          37.2          47.8         83.4       37.5        ✓
                           + no critic                  25.2          37.5          50.0         82.7       31.8        ✓
                            + low effort                24.5          37.3          47.8         82.5       29.8        ✓

                 The Agentic Scientist: Orchestrating Coding Agents for Automated Scientific (Co-)Discovery



8. Conclusion
Summary. Across multiple research case studies, The Agentic Scientist demonstrates that
coding agents can contribute to scientific research when embedded in an orchestrated workflow that
preserves evidence and tests claims. Our results span several levels of scientific autonomy, including a
co-created Error Diffusion paper accepted at a conference, the fully autonomous discovery of structural
inductive biases for Doc-to-LoRA, and a concrete CMA-anchored learned optimizer. Complementing
these detailed case studies, our cross-harness and model evaluation comprises 70 research campaigns
across five manually prepared tasks and 14 harness-model configurations. The manuscript AI reviews
show differences across model generations, and a positive trend toward newer models. With the
same LLM base model, we observe no consistent differences across harnesses. Stronger task search
performance predicts higher AI reviewer scores on 4 of 5 considered tasks. This positions The
Agentic Scientist as a human-steerable scaffold for end-to-end computational research.

Discussion. The Agentic Scientist and its experiments raise questions about the nature of
scientific contributions on several levels: for the researcher, what to specify and how much autonomy
to grant; for the generated artifacts, what a search score misses and how to validate generated
research. We discuss each below:
From implementation to specification. As coding agents take over implementation, the human role
shifts toward specifying the research contract: the task, its evaluators, and the rubrics that gate each
stage. This contract is also where failures can arise. Agents may exploit what the contract leaves
unstated: AI Scientist-v1 edited its code to extend its own time limits (Lu et al., 2024b), and The
Agentic Scientist changed the evaluation protocol on a subset of RL fine-tuning runs (Figure 16).
Furthermore, agents can follow the contract instructions too literally (Section 6.3), requiring a subtle
balance of scientific expression and concrete verifiability. Since neither deviation is apparent from the
reported numbers alone, precise specification and auditing become central research skills.
Autonomy spectrum. The AI Scientist-v2 produced a fully AI-generated paper that passed peer
review at a workshop before its pre-agreed withdrawal (Yamada et al., 2025). The Error Diffusion
paper, on the other hand, resulted from using The Agentic Scientist as a tool within a co-
scientist setting. The two modes are complementary: fully autonomous campaigns cheaply screen
many directions, while human steering turns the most promising ones into verified contributions.
In the Error Diffusion project, this human effort was supplied throughout: defining the direction,
baseline, and initial rubrics; verifying results, references, and claims; adding reinforcement-learning
experiments; and clarifying notation and mechanisms after peer review (Section 4).
Beyond hill-climbing. A higher search score often does not by itself imply a better scientific contribution.
Although Figure 9 shows that higher task scores tend to come with higher AI reviewer scores, the
score hides two aspects of scientific contributions. First, a single search objective can hide trade-offs:
a richer sparse autoencoder encoder reduces reconstruction error but worsens a feature-perturbation
metric, and a tabular deployment recipe raises balanced accuracy while lowering ordinary accuracy.
Second, a score gain can come from low-level engineering rather than high-level innovation: in RL
fine-tuning of language models, gains came from re-implementing and combining existing techniques
rather than from new mechanisms (Section 6.3). Post-search ablations, analysis, and reporting expose
the first; the second requires a provenance map that links ideas, hypotheses, and claims to prior work,
extending the current claim-to-source map beyond experimental evidence.
Validating generated research. We evaluate research campaigns with several signals of different
strength, each limited when viewed in isolation: AI reviewer scores, post-search transfer evaluations
(such as unseen Brax environments in Section 6.2), and external peer review. Peer review most
directly assesses scientific merit but needs expert time for every manuscript, so it cannot cover all


                                                                                                              21
                 The Agentic Scientist: Orchestrating Coding Agents for Automated Scientific (Co-)Discovery



campaigns, while AI reviewer scores can potentially be overfit like any other metric. Post-search
transfer evaluations offer a scalable middle ground, provided they are fixed before search and held out
from it. Other signals could strengthen this evidence: Reviewers drawn from several model families
to dilute the circularity of using the same models for investigation and rubric review (Zheng et al.,
2023); independent reproduction experiments behind each claim; and a measure of idea novelty,
which previous signals do not capture.

Limitations. At present, The Agentic Scientist could be enhanced by a reliable literature-to-
code pipeline for constructing state-of-the-art baselines. The task drafting pipeline (Section 3.1) is also
not fully robust: it can draft simple tasks such as circle packing from a research brief, but for larger
tasks such as the world-model and tabular distribution-shift tasks in Section 5, it may fail to produce
an initial program, evaluators, and task contract that exactly match the full Agentic Scientist
scaffold. Reliable task drafting nevertheless remains an important capability, since it removes the last
manual step between a research brief and an automated campaign. Unlike AI Scientist-v1 and AI
Scientist-v2 (Lu et al., 2024b; Yamada et al., 2025), The Agentic Scientist only explicitly
searches the literature during manuscript writing rather than during initial ideation. That being said,
we repeatedly observe that coding agents leverage general web access to inform idea investigation.
Beyond these capability gaps, the system’s verification is only as complete as its task-specific evaluators
and rubrics, which may omit methodological errors or reward unintended shortcuts. Results that
pass verification should therefore still be inspected and validated by human researchers. Although
the cross-harness and model evaluation covers 70 campaigns, its paper-level metric is an AI-reviewer
proxy rather than full evidence of scientific correctness, and only the Error Diffusion paper underwent
external peer review. Our evaluations are also restricted to computational machine learning, so
they do not establish the feasibility of transferring the orchestration workflow to domains requiring
physical experiments or human-subject studies. Finally, for autoillustrate we present additional
component-specific limitations and failure cases in Section E.3.

Ethical considerations. AI-assisted generation of research can create risks for attribution, research
integrity, and an already constrained peer-review process. We submitted only the single Error
Diffusion paper for external review after human verification; the other generated manuscripts served
as internal evaluation artifacts. For the Error Diffusion paper specifically, human researchers verified
its implementations, results, claims, and references, correcting unsupported claims and incomplete
attribution before submission. Responsible deployment also requires sandboxed execution, provenance
for generated code and text, disclosure of agent contributions, watermarking of generated papers,
and human accountability for safety, authorship, and final claims. More broadly, researchers remain
responsible for disclosing agent contributions and deciding whether generated work is suitable for
submission and external review.

Future work. Scientific progress often comes from exploring diverse “lottery tickets” across seem-
ingly unrelated domains. Applying The Agentic Scientist broadly could accelerate this process
by screening a larger hypothesis space through greater compute within individual research directions
and exploration of more directions within a topic. To support human validation, the claim-to-source
map should be exposed through an interface that lets researchers navigate from each claim to the
experiments and results behind it. The interface should also allow researchers to provide domain
expertise and use the system as an interactive research tool. Another improvement would be to
automate the design of post-search transfer evaluations. Where possible, rubric criteria should be
implemented as automated tests, checked for reward hacking, and complemented by independent
scientific review (Goel et al., 2026; Gunjal et al., 2026). With traceable evidence and enhanced
rubrics, The Agentic Scientist could broaden and accelerate experimental search while leaving
scientific judgment and responsibility with researchers.

                The Agentic Scientist: Orchestrating Coding Agents for Automated Scientific (Co-)Discovery



A. Additional Related Work
From fixed search spaces to long-running research agents. Computational discovery has tra-
ditionally been posed as search through a hypothesis space chosen in advance by the system’s
designers (Langley, 2024). Expressing the search space as code relaxes this constraint. Programs
written by language models have specified the learning problem, as training objectives (Lu et al.,
2024a), rewards (Ma et al., 2024), and environments (Faldor et al., 2025b), as well as the learner, as
network architectures (Chen et al., 2023), robot designs (Lehman et al., 2023), and agent designs (Hu
et al., 2025). At the scale of a research project, AIDE runs tree search over machine-learning engi-
neering code (Jiang et al., 2025), and AgentRxiv lets separate research agents build on one another’s
results through a shared preprint server (Schmidgall and Moor, 2025). Several concurrent systems
push further, sustaining research over long horizons or running many agents on a single problem.
DeepScientist (Weng et al., 2026) and InternAgent-1.5 (Feng et al., 2026) run a long-horizon research
loop over a cumulative memory, with DeepScientist promoting only findings that survive verification;
InternAgent-1.5 and EvoScientist (Lyu et al., 2026) further distill what worked and what failed into
reusable memories. AutoScientists (Gao et al., 2026), CORAL (Qu et al., 2026a), and Agora (Zhang
et al., 2026b) instead run many agents at once and coordinate them through shared state rather than
a central planner: self-organizing teams around a shared experimental record, asynchronous agents
in isolated workspaces with a separate evaluator, and workers appending results and verifications to
a Git graph. Our workers likewise explore in separate workspaces and exchange distilled conclusions
at cycle boundaries; in addition, each cycle is bound to a typed contract, and the selected program is
then studied through controlled baselines and ablations.

Judging ideas, rubrics, and evidence auditing. Deciding which ideas and results to trust is a
recurring difficulty. Language models can propose ideas grounded in the literature (Baek et al.,
2025; Wang et al., 2024a), and lightweight graph-based evaluators (Feng et al., 2025) or LLM judges
that track human preferences (Zheng et al., 2023) can score them. Other work anchors results to
something checkable: RE-Bench compares agents with human experts on research engineering (Wijk
et al., 2025), and data-to-paper traces every result in a manuscript back through the code to the
data (Ifargan et al., 2025). Final scores and papers can still conceal how they were obtained. An
independent evaluation of The AI Scientist found weak novelty checks, failed experiments, and poorly
substantiated manuscripts behind its claims (Beel et al., 2025), and controlled audits of two open-
source systems found biased benchmark selection, post-hoc selection on test results, and undisclosed
subsampled or synthetic data, flaws that an LLM auditor detected far more reliably from logs and
code than from the paper alone (Luo et al., 2026). Closest to our contracted research cycles, Xcientist
specifies at each stage the implementation, comparisons, and artifacts a claim requires and retains
the source evidence behind it; a retrospective audit then measures claim drift, that is, unresolved
mismatches between accepted claims and their sources, code, or experiments (Wang et al., 2026).
Verification can also be delegated to models: Gemini has been used to hunt for flaws in proofs and
to write and run code that checks derivations (Woodruff et al., 2026), and auto-generated rubrics
have served as the reward for post-training an AI scientist agent on paper replication (Falck et al.,
2026). In The Agentic Scientist, the ideation agent fixes the target rubrics before execution,
and retrospective ablations isolate each change introduced by the selected program.

Grounded paper writing. Language models already take on parts of the writing and reviewing pro-
cess: AutoSurvey drafts literature surveys (Wang et al., 2024b), and GPT-4 feedback on manuscripts
overlaps with human reviews about as much as two human reviewers overlap with each other (Liang
et al., 2024). Full-paper writers differ in how tightly they bind text to evidence. PaperOrchestra
writes a manuscript from whatever materials it is given, without being tied to a particular experiment
pipeline (Song et al., 2026), whereas Spark-to-Paper, a set of skills inside a coding assistant, decides

                                                                                                             35
                The Agentic Scientist: Orchestrating Coding Agents for Automated Scientific (Co-)Discovery



which evidence an experiment must produce before running it and checks the draft both deterministi-
cally and by self-critique (Qian et al., 2026). Checks on the finished paper are needed too. ReAgent
compares an agent-written paper with its repository, reading the code for the described method
and rerunning experiments for the reported numbers (Shen et al., 2026), and MLReplicate shows
why: when experts reviewed the ten agent-generated manuscripts an automated review pipeline had
accepted, 59% of their reviews flagged fabricated or unsupported claims (Gaddipati et al., 2026).
Our writing phase similarly maps each claim to its source artifacts, and runs an independent audit
agent in every round of revision.

Scientific figure generation. Beyond raster pipelines such as PaperBanana (Zhu et al., 2026a), a line
of work treats a figure as a program that can be revised after generation. DeTikZify synthesizes TikZ
from sketches with a fine-tuned vision-language model (Belouadi et al., 2024); recent agentic systems
instead start from a paper’s text. AutoFigure iterates an SVG layout under a critic but renders the
final figure with an image model from a text prompt derived from that layout (Zhu et al., 2026b), and
AutoFigure-Edit and CraftEditor recover editability by tracing the rendered raster into SVG (Lin et al.,
2026; Zhao et al., 2026). LiveFigure has a VLM write python-pptx code with explicit coordinates
and counts the manual edits needed to reach publication quality (Shao et al., 2026), while FigTree
recursively decomposes a figure into SVG fragments refined by render-critic loops (Liu et al., 2026).
SciFig splits generation into planning, layout synthesis, component rendering, and VLM-guided
refinement, and outputs XML diagrams that remain editable in standard diagramming tools (Huang
et al., 2026a). In all of these, geometry is delegated to the image model or emitted directly by the
language model, and checked only after the fact, visually or with lightweight overlap heuristics, rather
than enforced by construction. autoillustrate instead has agents write coordinate-free draw.io
templates and Python layout programs that must pass deterministic collision and routing checks, and
confines the image model to a final restyle audited against the deterministic render.




                                                                                                             36
                 The Agentic Scientist: Orchestrating Coding Agents for Automated Scientific (Co-)Discovery



B. The Agentic Scientist: Additional Results
B.1. Details on Aggregated Sweep Task Discoveries
To illustrate the discoveries behind these aggregate scores, we highlight the manuscript with the
highest AI reviewer score for each task, breaking ties by final task performance (Figure 19).




Figure 19 | First pages of the five highlighted task manuscripts, selected by highest AI reviewer score
and final task performance. The generated papers are watermarked by The Agentic Scientist.


Learned optimization. Claude Code with Opus 5 broadens meta-training from clean dense-network
tasks to a composite task that averages dense and convolutional network losses, with per-particle
label corruption. It also adds horizon conditioning and probe-based checkpoint selection. Ablations
attribute the development gains primarily to task diversity and label corruption, with little benefit from
the latter additions. On sealed SVHN, performance remains near chance, and the small advantage
over the reference mainly reflects one avoided divergence rather than better typical updates.

Sparse autoencoders. Pi with Opus 5 replaces a one-shot TopK encoder with two residual-pursuit
rounds followed by four learned sparse-code refinement steps, while preserving the sparsity budget.
Intermediate reconstruction losses and changes to dictionary optimization accompany this richer
encoder. Against matched TopK and BatchTopK references, held-out reconstruction error falls by
28.8–53.8%, but fitting takes approximately 40× longer. A targeted feature-perturbation metric
worsens, so the reconstruction gains do not establish improved interpretability.

Tabular distribution shift. OpenCode with Opus 5 augments a TabICLv2 stage-one baseline trained
from scratch with support-fitted rescaling and clipping, feature-permutation and class-rotation ensem-
bling, and posterior correction using a class prior estimated from query predictions. Class-balanced
training loss is also implemented, but ablations find no measurable benefit from that change. The
deployment recipe raises sealed mean balanced accuracy from 0.606 to 0.778, while ordinary accuracy
falls from 0.781 to 0.766, exposing a trade-off between macro-recall and aggregate accuracy.

World models. OpenCode with DeepSeek Flash aligns TinyWorlds training and decoding with
next-frame prediction: four clean context frames condition a fully masked fifth frame, and one
prediction pass replaces iterative MaskGIT decoding. The selected model also enlarges the dynamics
transformer and quantizer codebook. On sealed Pong, its mean relative rollout-error reduction against
the reference is 49%. Ablations support the training and decoding changes, while removing the
selected model’s constant-velocity latent initializer improves development results. Evidence remains
limited to action-free Pong and one seed per evaluation setting.



                                                                                                              37
                 The Agentic Scientist: Orchestrating Coding Agents for Automated Scientific (Co-)Discovery



Hyperparameter optimization. Claude Code with Opus 5 replaces the incumbent-centred local-
search baseline with a Gaussian-process optimizer using Expected Improvement. It selects a loss
transformation per problem, averages over kernel hyperparameters for mixed search spaces, and
refines acquisition candidates through trust-region proposals and coordinate search. Ablations
identify target transformation as the largest measured contributor. Gains are strongest on mixed
and hierarchical spaces; the comparisons match query counts rather than evaluation cost and do not
establish superiority over differential evolution on continuous tasks.

B.3. Error-Diffusion Additional Results
Bias offsets and Dale-style sign preservation
Because biases are unconstrained and may become negative, we ask whether bias offsets are large
enough to functionally dominate the synaptic preactivation terms, which would weaken the interpre-
tation of the learned activity as primarily shaped by Dale-style synaptic interactions.
We decomposed each preactivation as
                                                      𝑧 = 𝑧syn + 𝑏,
and measured 𝔼| 𝑏 |/𝔼| 𝑧syn |, the fraction of elements for which | 𝑏 | > | 𝑧syn |, and the fraction for which
adding 𝑏 flips the preactivation sign. Across all 5000 validation examples, bias effects were concen-
trated almost entirely in the first convolutional layer: conv1_p had a bias/synaptic ratio of 0.390, with
biases dominating 30.5% of preactivation elements and flipping 14.9% of signs; conv1_n showed
a similar ratio of 0.379, with 31.2% bias-dominant elements and a 15.2% sign-flip rate. In deeper
layers, bias effects were negligible: conv2 and conv3 had bias/synaptic ratios at most 0.0105 and
sign-flip rates below 0.004, while all fully connected layers had ratios at most 0.0032 and sign-flip
rates below 0.001.
These results suggest that negative biases primarily act as threshold shifts, especially in the first
convolutional layer, rather than as a mechanism for violating Dale-style synaptic signs. The formal
pathway-level excitatory and inhibitory structure remains intact, while bias offsets provide a limited
stream-specific adjustment to activation thresholds.

      Problem setup
